Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: alg:none and algorithm-confusion attacks, skipping signature verification, not checking audience (token replay across services), long-lived tokens that can't be revoked, and putting sensitive data in a readable payload. Expect "how do you validate a JWT" and "what's the alg:none attack."
7.  Troubleshooting: A JWT is rejected, decode it (it's just base64url), check the kid resolves to a current JWKS key, and verify iss/aud/exp. If a forged token was accepted, the verifier wasn't pinning the algorithm or checking the signature.
8.  Say it in the room: "A JWT is a signed, base64url container of claims, header, payload, signature. It's integrity-protected, not encrypted, so I never put secrets in the payload. I validate it fully every time: resolve the key from JWKS by kid, verify the signature with a pinned asymmetric algorithm, and check iss, aud, exp, and nbf. The classic attacks I guard against are alg:none and algorithm confusion, and because JWTs are hard to revoke I keep lifetimes short and pair them with refresh."
9.  Likely follow-ups: What's the alg:none / algorithm-confusion attack? How do you revoke a JWT before it expires? Why check audience, and what happens if you don't?

## 31. JWS and JWE

1.  What it is: JWS (JSON Web Signature) provides integrity and authenticity, signed, readable. JWE (JSON Web Encryption) provides confidentiality, encrypted so only the intended recipient can read it. A JWT is usually a JWS; when the content must be hidden, you use JWE (sometimes a signed-then-encrypted nested token).
2.  Why it matters: Knowing when you need encryption versus just a signature is a real design decision. Interviewers probe whether you understand that a signed JWT leaks its claims and when that's unacceptable (PII, sensitive scopes).
3.  How it works: JWS signs the payload (HMAC or asymmetric) so recipients can verify integrity and origin, but anyone can read it. JWE encrypts the payload to the recipient's public key (or a shared key), so only the holder of the corresponding key can decrypt. Nested JWT = sign with JWS, then encrypt the result with JWE, giving both properties.
4.  Architecture and flow: JWS, issuer signs, verifier checks signature, payload visible. JWE, issuer encrypts to recipient key, only recipient decrypts. Nested, sign then encrypt.
5.  How I would configure it: Default to JWS for tokens where claims aren't sensitive. Use JWE when the token must traverse parties that shouldn't read it or carries confidential data. For maximum assurance use a nested signed-then-encrypted token, and manage both signing and encryption keys with rotation.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Assuming a signed JWT is confidential, key management complexity for JWE, and choosing weak algorithms. Expect "difference between JWS and JWE" and "when would you encrypt a token instead of just signing it."
7.  Troubleshooting: A JWE can't be read, confirm the recipient has the correct decryption key and algorithm. A JWS trust failure is a signature/key problem. Nested tokens require validating both layers in order.
8.  Say it in the room: "JWS is signed, integrity and authenticity but readable; JWE is encrypted, confidentiality for the intended recipient only. Most JWTs are JWS, and the mistake I watch for is treating a signed token as if it were private, it isn't, so no PII or secrets in a JWS payload. When the claims are sensitive or pass through parties that shouldn't see them, I use JWE, or a nested sign-then-encrypt token for both properties."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Is a standard JWT confidential? Why or why not? When do you need JWE over JWS? What's a nested JWT and why use one?

## 32. X.509 certificates

1.  What it is: X.509 is the standard format for public-key certificates, binding a public key to a subject identity, signed by a Certificate Authority. They're the basis of TLS, mutual TLS, code signing, S/MIME, and certificate-based authentication like smart cards and PIV/CAC.
2.  Why it matters: Certificates are foundational and their lifecycle is a constant source of outages and audit findings (expiry, weak chains, revocation gaps). For ICAM especially, PIV/CAC certificate auth is central. Interviewers probe validation and lifecycle.
3.  How it works: A CA signs a certificate asserting that a public key belongs to a subject (with SANs, key usage/EKU, validity dates). A relying party validates the chain to a trusted root, checks the hostname/subject, key usage, validity period, and revocation status (CRL or OCSP) before trusting it. Possession is proven by a signature with the matching private key.
4.  Architecture and flow: Subject generates a key pair and CSR, CA validates and signs, certificate deployed, relying party validates chain, name, usage, validity, and revocation on use.
5.  How I would configure it: Protect private keys (HSM or secure store), use appropriate key usage/EKU and SANs, keep validity periods short and automate renewal (ACME where possible), and ensure revocation checking actually works. Monitor expiry proactively, expired certs are the top avoidable outage.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Expiry (the classic outage), broken or incomplete chains, missing SANs, no revocation checking, and compromised private keys. Expect "walk me through certificate validation" and "how do you handle revocation."
7.  Troubleshooting: A TLS or cert-auth failure, check the chain to a trusted root, the subject/SAN match, validity dates, key usage, and revocation. The error usually names which check failed (untrusted issuer, expired, name mismatch).
8.  Say it in the room: "X.509 binds a public key to an identity, signed by a CA, and it's the basis for TLS, mTLS, and PIV/CAC smart-card auth. On use, the relying party validates the chain to a trusted root, the name, the key usage, validity dates, and revocation via CRL or OCSP. Operationally the whole game is lifecycle, I automate renewal and monitor expiry aggressively because an expired cert is the most common and most avoidable outage, and I keep private keys in an HSM or secure store."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Walk me through the steps of certificate validation. CRL versus OCSP for revocation, trade-offs? How do you avoid certificate-expiry outages at scale?

## 33. FIDO2 and WebAuthn

1.  What it is: FIDO2 (WebAuthn + CTAP) is the standard for phishing-resistant, public-key authentication. Instead of a shared secret, the authenticator holds a private key and signs a challenge; the relying party verifies with the stored public key. Nothing phishable ever leaves the device.
2.  Why it matters: It's the strongest widely deployable authentication and the foundation of passkeys. Interviewers want to hear why it's phishing-resistant (origin binding) and how the registration and authentication ceremonies work.
3.  How it works: On registration, the relying party sends a challenge with its rpId; the authenticator creates a new key pair scoped to that origin, confirms user presence/verification, and returns the public key plus attestation. On authentication, the RP sends a fresh challenge; the authenticator signs it along with the origin and a signature counter; the RP verifies the signature against the stored public key and checks the origin and counter. Because the key is bound to the exact origin, a phishing site can't use it.
4.  Architecture and flow: Browser plus authenticator, RP challenge with rpId, navigator.credentials.create (register) or .get (authenticate), signed assertion with authenticatorData and clientDataJSON, RP verifies signature, origin, challenge, and signCount.
5.  How I would configure it: Set rpId to the registrable domain and restrict allowed origins, require userVerification for high-assurance, store public key and sign counter per credential, and verify origin, challenge, signature, and counter increase on every login. Support both platform and roaming authenticators.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Account recovery (the hard problem, what if the authenticator is lost), attestation handling, and cross-device/roaming nuances. Expect "why is FIDO2 phishing-resistant" and "how do you handle a lost authenticator."
7.  Troubleshooting: Authentication fails, check that the origin and rpId match exactly, the challenge is fresh, the signature verifies against the stored key, and the sign counter advanced (a stalled or regressed counter can indicate a cloned authenticator).
8.  Say it in the room: "FIDO2/WebAuthn is public-key authentication, the authenticator signs a challenge with a private key that never leaves the device, and the RP verifies with the stored public key. It's phishing-resistant because the credential is bound to the exact origin, so a lookalike site can't trigger a valid signature. I enforce user verification for high assurance and check origin, challenge, signature, and the sign counter each time. The real design challenge isn't the crypto, it's recovery when someone loses their authenticator, so I plan for registered backups."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Why exactly is FIDO2 phishing-resistant? How do you handle account recovery when an authenticator is lost? What's the role of attestation, and do you always need it?

## 34. Passkeys

1.  What it is: Passkeys are FIDO2/WebAuthn credentials made usable at consumer scale, discoverable (resident) credentials that sync across a user's devices through a platform provider (Apple iCloud Keychain, Google Password Manager) or stay device-bound on a security key. They're the user-friendly face of FIDO2 meant to replace passwords.
2.  Why it matters: Passkeys are how passwordless actually reaches the masses, so CIAM and workforce roadmaps both care. Interviewers probe the synced-versus-device-bound distinction and the security trade-off that syncing introduces.
3.  How it works: Same WebAuthn ceremonies as FIDO2, but the credential is discoverable (the user doesn't type a username) and, for synced passkeys, backed up and restored across the user's devices via the platform's encrypted sync. Device-bound passkeys (security keys) don't sync and offer the highest assurance.
4.  Architecture and flow: User registers a passkey, platform syncs it (encrypted) across their devices or keeps it on the key, login uses WebAuthn get() with a discoverable credential, RP verifies as with FIDO2.
5.  How I would configure it: Support passkeys as a primary passwordless method, decide whether your assurance needs allow synced passkeys (great UX, convenient recovery) or require device-bound keys (higher assurance, harder recovery). Communicate the backup/sync model to users and still plan recovery paths.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: The security trade-off of synced passkeys (the sync account becomes part of the attack surface), cross-ecosystem portability, and account recovery. Expect "synced versus device-bound passkeys" and "is a synced passkey good enough for high-assurance access."
7.  Troubleshooting: A user can't use their passkey on a new device, confirm whether it's a synced passkey (should restore via the platform account) or device-bound (needs re-registration). Many issues are really platform-sync or ecosystem-portability issues.
8.  Say it in the room: "Passkeys are WebAuthn credentials productized for consumers, discoverable, usually synced across a user's devices by Apple or Google, or device-bound on a security key. They give you phishing-resistant, passwordless login with good UX and easier recovery. The trade-off I flag is that synced passkeys move trust into the user's platform account, so for the highest assurance I'd require device-bound keys; for broad consumer CIAM, synced passkeys are the right balance."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Synced versus device-bound passkeys, when do you require each? Does syncing weaken the security model? How do passkeys change your account-recovery design?

## 35. PKI and certificate lifecycle

1.  What it is: PKI is the whole system of CAs, intermediates, policies, and processes that issue, manage, and revoke the certificates underpinning trust. The certificate lifecycle, enrollment, issuance, deployment, renewal, revocation, is the operational heart of it.
2.  Why it matters: PKI done badly means outages (expiry), weak trust (bad chains), and ungoverned keys. For ICAM, PKI is the basis of PIV/CAC credentialing. Interviewers probe trust hierarchy design and lifecycle automation.
3.  How it works: A root CA (offline, tightly protected) signs intermediate CAs that issue end-entity certificates, so the root's exposure is minimized. Certificates are enrolled via CSR (or ACME/SCEP/EST), issued per policy, deployed, monitored, renewed before expiry, and revoked (CRL/OCSP) when compromised or decommissioned.
4.  Architecture and flow: Offline root CA, intermediate issuing CAs, enrollment (CSR/ACME/SCEP), issuance, deployment, monitoring and renewal, revocation via CRL/OCSP.
5.  How I would configure it: Keep the root offline and protect keys in HSMs, use intermediates for issuance, automate enrollment and renewal (ACME) wherever possible, publish and monitor revocation, and maintain an inventory so no certificate expires unnoticed. Define certificate policies and validity periods deliberately.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Expiry from no inventory/automation, a compromised CA (catastrophic), broken chains from missing intermediates, and revocation that nobody actually checks. Expect "how do you design a trust hierarchy" and "a CA is compromised, what now."
7.  Troubleshooting: Trust failures, check the full chain is present and valid to a trusted root, the cert isn't expired or revoked, and the issuing intermediate is trusted. Mass expiry usually means the renewal automation or inventory failed.
8.  Say it in the room: "PKI is the trust machinery, an offline, HSM-protected root signing intermediates that issue end-entity certs, so the root is rarely exposed. The lifecycle is where it lives or dies: I automate enrollment and renewal with ACME, keep a certificate inventory so nothing expires by surprise, and make sure revocation is published and actually checked. A compromised issuing CA means revoke and re-issue down the chain, which is exactly why you isolate the root."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Why keep the root CA offline and use intermediates? How do you prevent certificate-expiry outages across thousands of certs? What's your response if an intermediate CA key is compromised?

## 36. TLS and mutual TLS

1.  What it is: TLS secures a channel, authenticating the server to the client and encrypting traffic. Mutual TLS (mTLS) adds client-certificate authentication so both ends prove identity, commonly used for service-to-service auth, zero-trust networking, and high-assurance APIs.
2.  Why it matters: TLS is table stakes; mTLS is a strong, certificate-based way to authenticate workloads and is central to service mesh and zero-trust designs. Interviewers probe the handshake, what each side validates, and where mTLS fits versus token-based auth.
3.  How it works: In the TLS handshake the server presents its certificate; the client validates the chain, hostname, validity, and revocation, and they negotiate keys. In mTLS the server also requests the client's certificate, and the client proves possession of its private key, so both identities are verified before any application data flows.
4.  Architecture and flow: Client connects, server presents cert (client validates), for mTLS server requests client cert (server validates), both prove key possession, encrypted channel established.
5.  How I would configure it: Modern TLS versions and strong ciphers, validate the full chain and hostname, and for mTLS issue short-lived client certs from a dedicated CA, map the client cert's identity to an authorization decision, and rotate aggressively. In a mesh, let the infrastructure (e.g. sidecars) handle mTLS and rotation.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Certificate expiry on either side, incomplete chains, hostname mismatch, and the operational burden of client-cert lifecycle at scale. Expect "mTLS versus bearer tokens for service auth" and "walk me through the mTLS handshake."
7.  Troubleshooting: An mTLS connection fails, determine which side's validation failed, server rejecting the client cert (chain/expiry/mapping) versus client rejecting the server cert. The handshake error usually points at the failing check.
8.  Say it in the room: "TLS authenticates the server and encrypts the channel; mTLS adds client-certificate auth so both ends prove identity, which is why it's a natural fit for service-to-service and zero-trust. The cost is client-cert lifecycle, so I use short-lived certs from a dedicated CA and automate rotation, typically pushing that into the mesh. Compared with bearer tokens, mTLS binds identity to the transport and is harder to replay, but tokens are easier to pass through layers, so I choose based on the trust boundary."
9.  Likely follow-ups: mTLS versus bearer tokens for service-to-service auth? How do you manage client-certificate rotation at scale? What does each side validate in an mTLS handshake?

## 37. HTTP Redirect and POST bindings

1.  What it is: In SAML, bindings define how protocol messages travel over HTTP. HTTP-Redirect encodes the message into URL query parameters (good for short requests); HTTP-POST puts it in a self-submitting form body (needed for larger, signed messages like assertions). The binding choice affects size limits and signing.
2.  Why it matters: Binding mismatches are a real cause of broken SAML, and understanding them signals you've actually debugged federation. Interviewers probe why assertions come back via POST and why AuthnRequests often go via Redirect.
3.  How it works: Redirect binding deflates and base64/URL-encodes the message into the URL, so it's constrained by URL length and typically used for the smaller AuthnRequest, with the signature as a separate query parameter. POST binding carries the (often larger, embedded-signature) Response/assertion in a hidden form field that the browser auto-submits to the endpoint.
4.  Architecture and flow: Redirect, message in URL query to the IdP SSO endpoint. POST, message in an auto-submitting HTML form to the SP's ACS endpoint.
5.  How I would configure it: Match the binding on both sides of the metadata, use Redirect for AuthnRequests and POST for Responses/assertions (which are too large and need embedded XML signatures), and make sure the configured endpoints correspond to the right binding.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Binding mismatch between IdP and SP, URL-length limits truncating a Redirect message, and signature placement differences between bindings. Expect "why is the assertion returned by POST and not Redirect."
7.  Troubleshooting: SAML fails at the transport level, confirm the IdP and SP agree on the binding per endpoint, check for URL truncation on Redirect, and verify the signature is where the binding expects it.
8.  Say it in the room: "SAML bindings are just how the message rides over HTTP. Redirect encodes it in the URL and suits the smaller AuthnRequest; POST uses an auto-submitting form and is used for the Response and assertion because they're larger and carry an embedded XML signature. Most binding bugs are a mismatch between the IdP and SP metadata, or a message too big for a Redirect URL, so I verify the binding per endpoint on both sides."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Why is the SAML Response delivered via POST rather than Redirect? What limits a Redirect-binding message? Where does the signature live in each binding?

## 38. Authorization Code flow

1.  What it is: The Authorization Code flow is OAuth's most secure interactive grant. The client gets a short-lived authorization code via a browser redirect, then exchanges it server-side at the token endpoint for tokens. The code goes through the browser; the tokens never do.
2.  Why it matters: It's the default correct choice for web apps (and, with PKCE, for SPAs and mobile). Interviewers probe why the two-step code exchange is more secure than handing a token straight to the browser (the deprecated implicit flow).
3.  How it works: The client redirects to /authorize with response_type=code, client_id, redirect_uri, scope, and state. The user authenticates and consents; the authorization server redirects back to the registered redirect_uri with a one-time code and the same state. The client then POSTs the code to /token with its credentials and the exact redirect_uri, and receives the access (and optionally refresh and ID) tokens.
4.  Architecture and flow: Client to /authorize (code request), user authenticates, redirect back with code and state, client to /token (code plus client auth), tokens returned.
5.  How I would configure it: Register exact redirect URIs, always use and verify the state parameter (CSRF defense), keep the code short-lived and single-use, authenticate confidential clients at the token endpoint, and add PKCE for public clients.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Missing state (CSRF), loose redirect-URI matching (open redirector/token theft), reusing or leaking the code, and using implicit flow instead. Expect "why is this more secure than implicit" and "what does state protect against."
7.  Troubleshooting: invalid_grant at the token endpoint, the code is expired, already used, or the redirect_uri doesn't match the one in the authorization request. A missing/mismatched state points at a CSRF or client bug.
8.  Say it in the room: "Authorization Code is OAuth's secure interactive grant: the browser only ever carries a short-lived, single-use code, and the actual token exchange happens server-side at the token endpoint with client authentication. That's why it beats the deprecated implicit flow, tokens don't ride in the URL. I enforce exact redirect URIs, a verified state parameter for CSRF, and single-use codes, and I add PKCE for any public client."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Why is Authorization Code more secure than implicit? What does the state parameter protect against? When do you add PKCE?

## 39. Authorization Code with PKCE

1.  What it is: PKCE (Proof Key for Code Exchange, RFC 7636) hardens the Authorization Code flow for clients that can't keep a secret, SPAs and mobile apps. It binds the authorization request to the token exchange with a one-time cryptographic proof, so a stolen code is useless to an attacker.
2.  Why it matters: PKCE is now recommended for all clients, not just public ones, and it's the modern answer to "how do you do OAuth in a SPA/mobile app safely." Interviewers want the code_verifier/code_challenge mechanism explained clearly.
3.  How it works: The client generates a random code_verifier and sends its hash as code_challenge (with method S256) on the /authorize request. When it later exchanges the code at /token, it includes the original code_verifier. The server hashes it and compares to the stored challenge; if they don't match, the exchange is rejected. So intercepting the code alone doesn't help, the attacker lacks the verifier.
4.  Architecture and flow: Client creates verifier and challenge, /authorize carries code_challenge (S256), code returned, /token carries code_verifier, server verifies hash matches, tokens issued.
5.  How I would configure it: Use S256 (never plain), generate a high-entropy verifier per request, and apply PKCE to every client including confidential ones. Combine with exact redirect URIs and state for defense in depth.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Using the plain challenge method, reusing verifiers, or assuming PKCE replaces client authentication for confidential clients (it complements it). Expect "walk me through PKCE" and "what attack does it stop."
7.  Troubleshooting: Token exchange fails with PKCE, confirm the verifier matches the challenge, the S256 method is used consistently, and the verifier is per-request and not reused. A mismatch means the verifier/challenge pairing broke.
8.  Say it in the room: "PKCE binds the authorization request to the token exchange so a stolen authorization code can't be redeemed by anyone else. The client sends a hashed code_challenge up front and proves it holds the original code_verifier at the token endpoint. It was created for public clients like SPAs and mobile that can't hold a secret, but it's now recommended for everyone. I always use S256 and a fresh high-entropy verifier per request."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Exactly what attack does PKCE prevent? Why S256 over the plain method? Do confidential clients still need PKCE?

## 40. Client Credentials flow

1.  What it is: The Client Credentials grant is OAuth for machine-to-machine access, no user involved. A service authenticates as itself and gets an access token to call an API under its own identity, for backend jobs, service-to-service calls, and automation.
2.  Why it matters: It's the right grant for non-human/workload access, and interviewers use it to check that you don't shoehorn user-centric flows into service scenarios, and that you manage the client's own credential securely.
3.  How it works: The client POSTs to /token with grant_type=client_credentials and authenticates (client secret, or better, a private-key JWT or mTLS). The authorization server validates the client and returns an access token scoped to what that client is allowed, with no user context and typically no refresh token (the client just re-requests).
4.  Architecture and flow: Confidential client to /token with client auth and grant_type=client_credentials, server validates client, access token (no user, no refresh) returned, token used against the API.
5.  How I would configure it: Prefer private-key JWT or mTLS client authentication over a shared secret, scope the token to least privilege, keep lifetimes short (re-request rather than refresh), and vault/rotate the client's credential like any other secret.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Leaked client secrets in code/config, over-broad scopes, and treating the token as a user token. Expect "which grant for service-to-service" and "how do you secure the client credential."
7.  Troubleshooting: A service call fails auth, check the client authenticated correctly (secret/JWT/mTLS), the token's scope and audience match the target API, and the token hasn't expired. invalid_client means the client auth itself failed.
8.  Say it in the room: "Client Credentials is machine-to-machine OAuth, no user, the service authenticates as itself and gets a least-privilege token. I avoid shared secrets where I can and use private-key JWT or mTLS for client authentication, keep the token short-lived and re-request rather than refresh, and manage the client's own credential in a vault with rotation, since that credential is now the keys to the kingdom for that workload."
9.  Likely follow-ups: When do you use Client Credentials versus a user flow? How do you authenticate the client more securely than a shared secret? Why is there usually no refresh token here?

## 41. Refresh tokens

1.  What it is: A refresh token is a long-lived credential the client uses to obtain new access tokens without making the user re-authenticate. It lets you keep access tokens short-lived (good for security) while preserving a smooth, long-lived session (good for UX).
2.  Why it matters: Refresh tokens are the pivot between security and usability, and they're sensitive because a stolen one grants ongoing access. Interviewers probe rotation, revocation, and detection of theft.
3.  How it works: Along with the access token, the authorization server issues a refresh token. When the access token expires, the client POSTs grant_type=refresh_token to get a new access token (and, with rotation, a new refresh token, invalidating the old one). Revoking the refresh token cuts off future access.
4.  Architecture and flow: Initial token response includes a refresh token, access token expires, client posts refresh_token to /token, new access token (and rotated refresh token) returned.
5.  How I would configure it: Use refresh-token rotation with reuse detection (if an old token is replayed, revoke the whole chain, that's a theft signal), bind the token to the client, keep access tokens short, and give refresh tokens a sensible maximum lifetime with revocation on logout or compromise.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Stolen refresh tokens granting persistent access, no rotation so a leaked token lives forever, and no revocation path. Expect "how do you detect and respond to a stolen refresh token" (rotation + reuse detection).
7.  Troubleshooting: Refresh fails with invalid_grant, the token was revoked, expired, already rotated (reuse), or issued to a different client. Reuse detection firing means a possible theft, investigate.
8.  Say it in the room: "Refresh tokens let me keep access tokens short-lived while the user stays logged in, I trade a single long-lived credential for many short ones. Because a stolen refresh token means ongoing access, I use rotation with reuse detection: each refresh issues a new token and invalidates the old, and if an old one is ever replayed I treat it as theft and revoke the whole chain. They're also my clean revocation point on logout or compromise."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How does refresh-token rotation with reuse detection work? How do you revoke access when a refresh token is stolen? Where should refresh tokens be stored in a SPA versus a mobile app?

## 42. Access tokens and ID tokens

1.  What it is: Two tokens with two jobs. The access token authorizes API calls (OAuth), it's for the resource server and often opaque or a scoped JWT. The ID token authenticates the user to the client (OIDC), it's always a JWT with identity claims and is for the client, not for calling APIs.
2.  Why it matters: Mixing them up is one of the most common OAuth/OIDC mistakes, sending an ID token to an API, or using an access token to identify the user. Interviewers use this to separate people who've read the spec from people who've only toggled settings.
3.  How it works: After authentication, the token endpoint returns an access token (presented as Bearer to the resource server, which checks scope/audience/expiry) and, with the openid scope, an ID token (validated by the client, signature, iss, aud, exp, nonce, to establish the user's identity). The audience makes the intended consumer explicit, access token's aud is the API; ID token's aud is the client.
4.  Architecture and flow: /token returns access token (aud = API) and ID token (aud = client), client validates the ID token to log the user in, client sends the access token to the API, API validates it.
5.  How I would configure it: Use the ID token only at the client to establish identity, use the access token only against resource servers, pin audiences so each token is accepted only by its intended consumer, and validate both appropriately. Don't put authorization scopes in the ID token or identity-proofing in the access token's job.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Sending the ID token to an API, using the access token to identify the user, and not validating audience. Expect "difference between an access token and an ID token, and who consumes each."
7.  Troubleshooting: An API rejects a token, confirm it's the access token (not the ID token) and that its audience and scope match the API. If the client can't identify the user, confirm it's validating the ID token, not reading the access token.
8.  Say it in the room: "Access token and ID token solve different problems. The access token authorizes API calls and is for the resource server, scope and audience checked. The ID token authenticates the user to the client and is a JWT I validate for signature, issuer, audience, expiry, and nonce. The mistake I guard against is crossing them, an ID token should never be sent to an API, and an access token should never be used to identify the user. Audiences keep each token pinned to its intended consumer."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Who consumes the ID token and who consumes the access token? What goes wrong if you send an ID token to a resource API? Why does audience matter for both?

## 43. SAML SP-initiated versus IdP-initiated SSO

1.  What it is: Two ways a SAML login can start. SP-initiated begins at the application, which sends an AuthnRequest to the IdP, the normal, more secure pattern. IdP-initiated begins at the IdP (e.g. an app launcher), which pushes an unsolicited assertion to the SP with no prior request.
2.  Why it matters: IdP-initiated has real security weaknesses (no InResponseTo to correlate, more replay/CSRF exposure) and interviewers probe whether you know why SP-initiated is preferred and when IdP-initiated is unavoidable.
3.  How it works: SP-initiated, the SP creates an AuthnRequest with a request ID, the IdP's response carries InResponseTo matching it, giving a correlation and anti-replay anchor plus RelayState to return the user to the original resource. IdP-initiated, the IdP sends an assertion unprompted; there's no AuthnRequest, so no InResponseTo to tie it to, and the SP must accept an unsolicited assertion, which widens the attack surface.
4.  Architecture and flow: SP-initiated, app to IdP (AuthnRequest), IdP to app (Response with InResponseTo). IdP-initiated, IdP to app (unsolicited Response, no InResponseTo).
5.  How I would configure it: Prefer SP-initiated everywhere you can. If IdP-initiated is required (portal launchers), tighten controls, strict audience and recipient validation, short assertion lifetimes, replay caches, and accept unsolicited assertions only from trusted IdPs.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: IdP-initiated replay and CSRF, inability to correlate without InResponseTo, and RelayState handling. Expect "why is SP-initiated more secure" and "when would you still use IdP-initiated."
7.  Troubleshooting: An IdP-initiated login fails or is rejected, check that the SP is configured to accept unsolicited assertions and that audience/recipient match, since there's no InResponseTo to lean on. For SP-initiated, verify the InResponseTo matches the original request ID.
8.  Say it in the room: "SP-initiated starts at the app with an AuthnRequest, so the response carries InResponseTo, which gives me correlation and replay protection, and RelayState returns the user to where they started. IdP-initiated starts at the IdP with an unsolicited assertion, there's no request to correlate against, so it's more exposed to replay and CSRF. I default to SP-initiated and only enable IdP-initiated for things like portal launchers, with tighter audience, recipient, lifetime, and replay controls."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Why is SP-initiated considered more secure? What does InResponseTo give you that IdP-initiated lacks? If you must support IdP-initiated, how do you harden it?

# IGA

## 44. SailPoint IdentityIQ and Identity Security Cloud architecture

1.  What it is: IdentityIQ (IIQ) is SailPoint's on-prem/self-hosted IGA platform; Identity Security Cloud (ISC, formerly IdentityNow) is the SaaS equivalent. Both govern the identity lifecycle, access requests, certifications, roles, and SoD, but IIQ is a Java app you run and extend, while ISC is multi-tenant SaaS with a lighter extension model.
2.  Why it matters: Interviewers want to know you can place the pieces, where identities, accounts, roles, and policies live, and that you understand the IIQ-versus-ISC trade-off (control and deep customization versus managed SaaS and faster time-to-value).
3.  How it works: IIQ runs on a Java app server over a relational database, aggregates from connected applications into the identity warehouse, builds identity cubes, and drives provisioning through connectors and workflows, with heavy customization via BeanShell rules. ISC does the same conceptually but as SaaS, using Virtual Appliances as the on-prem bridge to internal sources and a REST API plus transforms instead of arbitrary Java.
4.  Architecture and flow: Authoritative and application sources, aggregation into the identity warehouse, identity cubes correlated, roles/policies/certifications evaluated, provisioning through connectors (and VAs for ISC), with task results and audit underneath.
5.  How I would configure it: Start with the authoritative source and identity profile, onboard applications with clean schemas and correlation, define roles and policies, then layer certifications and lifecycle automation. In IIQ I'd budget for rule development and environment management; in ISC I'd lean on transforms and the API and accept the SaaS guardrails.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Over-customizing IIQ into an unmaintainable rule jungle, under-sizing the database, VA connectivity issues in ISC, and confusing which capabilities differ between the two. Expect "IIQ versus ISC, when do you choose each."
7.  Troubleshooting: Platform issues usually surface in task results and the application logs (IIQ) or the ISC activity/connectivity dashboards and VA logs. Isolate whether it's aggregation, correlation, provisioning, or a connector problem before touching config.
8.  Say it in the room: "SailPoint IdentityIQ is the self-hosted Java IGA platform; Identity Security Cloud is the SaaS version. Conceptually both do the same thing, aggregate accounts into an identity warehouse, build correlated identity cubes, and drive roles, certifications, SoD, and provisioning, but IIQ gives me deep BeanShell-level customization at the cost of running and maintaining it, while ISC trades some of that control for managed SaaS, transforms, and Virtual Appliances to reach internal sources. I choose IIQ for complex, highly-customized estates and ISC for speed and lower operational burden."
9.  Likely follow-ups: IIQ versus ISC, decision factors? What is a Virtual Appliance and why does ISC need it? Where does customization live in each?

## 45. Identity cube and identity warehouse

1.  What it is: The identity cube is SailPoint's unified model of a single person, correlating all their accounts, entitlements, roles, and attributes into one governable object. The identity warehouse is the aggregate store of all those cubes plus the account and entitlement data behind them, the single source of governance truth.
2.  Why it matters: Everything in IIQ, certifications, policy, requests, hangs off the cube. If you can explain the cube clearly, you show you understand how SailPoint turns scattered accounts into one reviewable identity. It's a signature SailPoint concept interviewers love.
3.  How it works: Aggregation pulls accounts and entitlements from each source into the warehouse; correlation links those accounts to an identity; the cube then presents the consolidated view, attributes from authoritative sources, all linked accounts, effective entitlements, role assignments, and policy status. Identity refresh recomputes the cube when inputs change.
4.  Architecture and flow: Sources aggregated into the warehouse, accounts correlated to identities, identity cube assembled (attributes, accounts, entitlements, roles, policy), refreshed on change.
5.  How I would configure it: Get the authoritative attributes and the correlation key right first, because the cube is only as good as its inputs. Define which attributes are searchable/governed, and schedule identity refresh so cubes reflect reality. Keep the warehouse performant as data grows.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Bad correlation splitting or merging cubes, stale cubes because refresh isn't scheduled, and warehouse performance at scale. Expect "what is an identity cube and what's in it."
7.  Troubleshooting: A cube looks wrong, check correlation (is an account linked to the right identity), the authoritative attribute mapping, and whether identity refresh has run since the change. Most cube issues are really correlation or refresh issues.
8.  Say it in the room: "The identity cube is SailPoint's single consolidated view of a person, their authoritative attributes plus every correlated account, entitlement, and role, and policy status. The identity warehouse is all the cubes and the underlying account/entitlement data. It's the backbone: certifications, requests, and policy all operate on the cube. So I obsess over correlation accuracy and scheduled identity refresh, because a cube built on bad correlation or stale data quietly corrupts everything downstream."
9.  Likely follow-ups: What exactly is contained in an identity cube? How does the cube get built and kept current? What happens when correlation is wrong?

## 46. Identity aggregation

1.  What it is: Identity aggregation is the task that reads accounts and their attributes from a connected source into SailPoint's warehouse. It's the "pull the current state of this application's accounts" operation that feeds correlation and the cube.
2.  Why it matters: It's the data-ingestion heartbeat of IGA. Interviewers probe the difference between aggregating accounts and building identities, and the trade-offs of full versus delta/partitioned aggregation at scale.
3.  How it works: A connector connects to the source, reads accounts per the application schema, and loads them into the warehouse as account objects. Full aggregation reads everything; delta aggregation reads only changes; partitioning splits large aggregations across threads. Aggregation itself doesn't correlate or refresh, those are related but separate steps.
4.  Architecture and flow: Scheduled aggregation task, connector reads accounts per schema, accounts loaded to warehouse, then correlation and identity refresh act on them.
5.  How I would configure it: Match the schema to the source accurately, use delta aggregation and partitioning for large sources, schedule frequency to match how fast the source changes, and watch connector credentials and performance. Pair aggregation with a reconciliation mindset so missed changes get caught.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Schema drift, connector credential/permission failures, long full aggregations timing out, and assuming aggregation also correlates or refreshes. Expect "aggregation versus identity refresh, what's the difference."
7.  Troubleshooting: Accounts missing or stale, check the aggregation task result for errors, connector connectivity and permissions, and whether delta missed a change (run a full aggregation to confirm). Then check correlation and refresh separately.
8.  Say it in the room: "Aggregation is the task that reads a source's accounts and attributes into SailPoint's warehouse, per the application schema. It's just ingestion, correlation links those accounts to identities and identity refresh rebuilds the cube afterward, so I keep those three steps mentally separate. At scale I use delta aggregation and partitioning and size the schedule to the source's change rate, and I read the task result first whenever accounts look wrong."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Full versus delta aggregation, when each? Does aggregation correlate accounts? How do you handle a source with millions of accounts?

## 47. Account aggregation and correlation

1.  What it is: Account aggregation reads the accounts; correlation decides which identity each account belongs to. Correlation uses configured rules (match on employeeId, UPN, email) to link accounts to cubes, and anything that doesn't match becomes an uncorrelated account to investigate.
2.  Why it matters: Correlation quality is the foundation of governance accuracy, and the uncorrelated pile is where orphans and rogue accounts surface. Interviewers probe how you avoid merging two people or splitting one, and what you do with uncorrelated accounts.
3.  How it works: After aggregation, correlation logic (attribute-based config or a correlation rule) compares account attributes to identity attributes and links on the best match. Strong, stable keys link cleanly; weak keys risk false matches. Uncorrelated accounts are flagged, and many become orphan or service accounts needing ownership.
4.  Architecture and flow: Accounts aggregated, correlation rules compare account-to-identity on a stable key, matches link to the cube, non-matches flagged as uncorrelated.
5.  How I would configure it: Correlate on the most stable attribute available with ordered fallback rules, never auto-link on weak attributes, and route ambiguous matches to review. Monitor the uncorrelated count as a health and audit signal.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Correlating on mutable/non-unique values causing merges or splits, and uncorrelated accounts left unmanaged. Expect "how do you prevent two identities from correlating to the same account" and "what do you do with uncorrelated accounts."
7.  Troubleshooting: An account correlated to the wrong identity, inspect the correlation rule and the attribute values on both sides. An account that won't correlate either lacks the key attribute or is genuinely ownerless (an orphan).
8.  Say it in the room: "Correlation is the step that attributes each aggregated account to the right identity cube, and it's where governance accuracy is won or lost. I correlate on the most stable key available with ordered fallbacks and send ambiguous matches to review rather than guessing, because a bad auto-correlation merges two people and is painful to unwind. The uncorrelated pile isn't noise, it's exactly where orphaned and service accounts show up, which is what audits care about."
9.  Likely follow-ups: What do you correlate on and what's your fallback order? How do you handle an account that matches two identities? What's the governance value of uncorrelated accounts?

## 48. Identity refresh

1.  What it is: Identity refresh is the IIQ task that recomputes the identity cube, re-running correlation, role assignment, policy evaluation, and attribute promotion so the cube reflects the latest aggregated data. It's the "make the cube current and consistent" operation.
2.  Why it matters: Without refresh, cubes go stale, role assignments and policy violations don't update, and certifications review outdated state. Interviewers probe the distinction between aggregation (bring data in) and refresh (recompute the identity), which trips up less experienced people.
3.  How it works: Refresh iterates identities and applies configured options, correlate accounts, promote authoritative attributes, assign roles, detect policy/SoD violations, process lifecycle state, and trigger provisioning where configured. You enable only the options you need for performance.
4.  Architecture and flow: Aggregated data in warehouse, identity refresh task runs selected options (correlate, promote attributes, assign roles, check policy), cubes updated and provisioning/events may fire.
5.  How I would configure it: Enable only necessary refresh options to keep it fast, schedule it after aggregation, and partition for scale. Be deliberate about which refreshes trigger provisioning, an unintended option can fan out a lot of changes.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Stale cubes from missing/infrequent refresh, slow refresh from enabling everything, and surprise provisioning from the wrong option. Expect "aggregation versus identity refresh" and "why didn't a role assignment take effect."
7.  Troubleshooting: A cube change didn't take (role, policy, attribute), confirm identity refresh ran with the relevant option enabled after the aggregation. Performance issues usually trace to too many options or no partitioning.
8.  Say it in the room: "Identity refresh recomputes the cube, correlation, attribute promotion, role assignment, policy and SoD evaluation, after aggregation brings the data in. The key distinction is that aggregation loads accounts and refresh makes the identity consistent, so if a role assignment or policy violation isn't updating, I check whether refresh ran with that option enabled. I keep it lean by turning on only the options I need and partitioning for scale, and I'm careful which options trigger provisioning."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Difference between aggregation and identity refresh? Which refresh options trigger provisioning? How do you keep refresh performant at scale?

## 49. Authoritative sources in SailPoint

1.  What it is: The authoritative source (in IIQ, the application flagged authoritative; in ISC, the identity profile's source) is the system that defines who exists and supplies the trusted identity attributes. It's what drives identity creation and the attribute values that flow to the cube.
2.  Why it matters: Get the authoritative source and its mapping wrong and every identity is wrong. Interviewers probe multi-source authority, precedence, and the immutable identifier, classic IGA design judgment.
3.  How it works: The authoritative source (usually HR) is aggregated, and its accounts create identities (not just link to them). Attribute mappings promote source fields into identity attributes. When multiple sources contribute, you define precedence. The immutable correlation key ties everything together across sources.
4.  Architecture and flow: HR/authoritative source aggregated, its records create identities, attributes promoted per mapping and precedence, other sources correlate accounts onto those identities.
5.  How I would configure it: Choose a truly authoritative source for existence, map a stable immutable key, define attribute precedence for multi-source cases, and handle non-employees (contractors) whose existence may come from a different authoritative feed. Validate that creation only happens from authoritative sources.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Non-authoritative apps accidentally creating identities, mutable keys causing merges, and contractors with no HR authority. Expect "what's your authoritative source and immutable identifier, and how do you handle non-employees."
7.  Troubleshooting: Duplicate or missing identities, check which source is flagged authoritative, the create logic, and the correlation key. Duplicates often mean a second source is creating instead of correlating.
8.  Say it in the room: "In SailPoint the authoritative source defines existence and supplies trusted attributes, usually HR driving identity creation. I map a stable immutable key for correlation and set precedence when multiple sources contribute attributes. The design judgment interviewers look for is handling non-employees, who often need a separate authoritative feed, and making sure only authoritative sources create identities while everything else merely correlates, otherwise you get duplicates."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How do you handle contractors with no HR record? Multiple sources disagree on an attribute, how do you resolve precedence? What makes a good immutable identifier?

## 50. Application onboarding

1.  What it is: Application onboarding is the end-to-end process of bringing a target system under IGA, choosing a connector, defining the schema, configuring aggregation and correlation, and enabling provisioning and governance for it. It's the repeatable unit of work that scales an IGA program.
2.  Why it matters: Onboarding quality determines whether an app is actually governed or just visible. Interviewers probe your onboarding method and how you prioritize which apps to onboard first (risk and coverage).
3.  How it works: Select the connector (direct, SCIM, web services, flat file as a fallback), map the account schema and entitlements, configure correlation to the identity, test aggregation read-only, then enable provisioning operations deliberately, and finally wire it into roles, requests, and certifications.
4.  Architecture and flow: Connector chosen, schema and entitlements mapped, correlation configured, read-only aggregation validated, provisioning enabled, app wired into governance (roles, requests, certs).
5.  How I would configure it: Onboard read-only first to validate aggregation and correlation before enabling any writes. Prioritize high-risk and widely-used apps. Use a least-privilege service account, document the schema and entitlement meanings, and only then turn on provisioning and certification.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Enabling provisioning before correlation is clean (dangerous writes), weak connectors forcing flat-file imports, and undocumented entitlements. Expect "walk me through onboarding an application" and "how do you prioritize what to onboard."
7.  Troubleshooting: Onboarding issues split cleanly, aggregation/connectivity, correlation accuracy, or provisioning. Validate each stage in order; never debug provisioning before aggregation and correlation are solid.
8.  Say it in the room: "Application onboarding is my repeatable unit for scaling IGA: pick the connector, map the schema and entitlements, configure correlation, validate aggregation read-only, then enable provisioning and wire it into roles, requests, and certifications. The discipline that matters is going read-only first, I never enable writes until correlation is clean, because provisioning on bad correlation does real damage. I prioritize by risk and user coverage so the program shows value and reduces risk fast."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Walk me through onboarding a new application. How do you prioritize which apps to onboard? Why validate read-only before enabling provisioning?

## 51. Application schemas and account attributes

1.  What it is: The application schema is SailPoint's definition of what an account on a target looks like, which attributes exist, which is the identity/account ID, which are entitlement attributes (groups, roles), and their types. It's the contract between the connector and the target's data.
2.  Why it matters: A wrong schema means wrong correlation, wrong entitlements, and failed provisioning. Interviewers probe how you identify the correlation key and entitlement attributes, and how you handle multi-valued attributes like group membership.
3.  How it works: The connector discovers or you define the schema, native identity (the account's unique key on the target), display attributes, and entitlement attributes (often multi-valued, like memberOf). Entitlement attributes become the governable entitlements; the identity attribute feeds correlation.
4.  Architecture and flow: Target schema defined/discovered, account ID and entitlement attributes identified, aggregation reads per schema, entitlements and correlation derive from the mapped attributes.
5.  How I would configure it: Identify the stable native identity and the real entitlement attributes, mark multi-valued entitlement attributes correctly, and only aggregate the attributes you'll govern. Keep the schema in sync with the target as it changes.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Wrong native identity (breaks correlation and provisioning), missing or mis-typed entitlement attributes, and schema drift. Expect "how do you model group membership" and "what's the account's unique identifier here."
7.  Troubleshooting: Entitlements not showing or provisioning failing, check the schema's entitlement attribute definitions and the native identity. Many provisioning failures are a schema/attribute mismatch with the target's actual API.
8.  Say it in the room: "The application schema is the account contract, the native identity key, the display attributes, and the entitlement attributes like group membership, which are usually multi-valued. I get the native identity right because it drives both correlation and provisioning, mark entitlement attributes correctly so groups become governable entitlements, and only aggregate what I'll actually govern. Schema drift on the target is a common silent breaker, so I keep it in sync."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How do you model multi-valued group membership? What is the native identity and why does it matter? What happens when the target changes its schema?

## 52. Identity mappings and transforms

1.  What it is: Mappings and transforms shape raw source data into clean identity attributes, concatenating names, normalizing case, deriving a username, computing a status from HR fields. In IIQ these are attribute mappings and rules; in ISC they're transforms (JSON-defined) and seaspray.
2.  Why it matters: Dirty inbound data is the number-one cause of bad identities, and transforms are how you clean it deterministically. Interviewers probe how you generate unique usernames, handle nulls, and keep transforms maintainable.
3.  How it works: During identity refresh (IIQ) or identity profile processing (ISC), each identity attribute is populated from a source attribute via a direct mapping or a transform/rule that manipulates the value. Transforms chain, so you can normalize, substring, lookup, and conditionally default values.
4.  Architecture and flow: Source attributes, mapping or transform applied, computed identity attribute populated on the cube, used downstream for correlation, roles, and provisioning.
5.  How I would configure it: Prefer declarative transforms over code where possible for maintainability, handle nulls and collisions explicitly (especially username generation, which must guarantee uniqueness), and test transforms against messy real data. Keep logic centralized rather than scattered.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Non-unique generated usernames, null-handling gaps, and transform logic that's impossible to maintain. Expect "how do you generate a unique username" and "transforms versus rules in ISC."
7.  Troubleshooting: An identity attribute is wrong, trace the mapping/transform for that attribute and the source value feeding it. Username collisions mean the uniqueness logic didn't account for duplicates.
8.  Say it in the room: "Mappings and transforms turn messy source data into clean, deterministic identity attributes, in ISC that's JSON transforms, in IIQ it's attribute mappings and rules. The classic hard case is username generation, which has to guarantee uniqueness and handle collisions and nulls gracefully. I favor declarative transforms over code for maintainability, centralize the logic, and test against real dirty data rather than the happy path."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How do you generate a guaranteed-unique username? Transforms versus rules in ISC, when do you drop to code? How do you handle null or missing source attributes?

## 53. Joiner, Mover, Leaver workflows in IGA

1.  What it is: The concrete implementation of JML inside the IGA tool, lifecycle state detection plus the workflows and provisioning that fire on each transition. It's where the lifecycle concept becomes real tasks, approvals, and connector operations.
2.  Why it matters: This is the core of an IGA engineer's job. Interviewers want to hear how you detect the transition (lifecycle event versus attribute change), what fires automatically versus requires approval, and how you guarantee leaver completeness.
3.  How it works: Lifecycle state is computed from authoritative attributes (e.g. status, start/end dates). A transition triggers a lifecycle event or workflow, joiner provisions birthright access, mover recalculates role-based access and revokes what no longer applies, leaver disables and deprovisions. Provisioning plans carry out the changes through connectors.
4.  Architecture and flow: Authoritative change, lifecycle state recomputed on refresh, lifecycle event/workflow fires, provisioning plan built, connectors execute, result reconciled.
5.  How I would configure it: Drive transitions off clean authoritative attributes, make joiner birthright role-based, make mover actively revoke stale access (not just add), and make leaver a fast disable followed by full deprovision with a measured SLA. Build in exception handling and notifications.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Movers that only grant, leaver deprovisioning that misses disconnected or manual apps, and lifecycle state computed from dirty data. Expect "how do you ensure a leaver loses all access" and "how do you handle the mover revoke problem."
7.  Troubleshooting: A lifecycle action didn't fire or complete, check whether refresh recomputed the state, whether the event/workflow triggered, and the provisioning plan and connector result. Gaps are usually in one connector or a manual-fulfillment app.
8.  Say it in the room: "In the IGA tool, JML is lifecycle-state detection plus workflows and provisioning plans. Joiner grants birthright access by role; mover recalculates and, crucially, revokes the stale entitlements; leaver does a fast disable then full deprovision against a measured SLA. The two things I watch hardest are movers that only add and never remove, which creates creep, and leaver completeness across disconnected or manually-fulfilled apps, which is where access quietly survives."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How do you guarantee leaver completeness across disconnected apps? How do you solve the mover revoke problem? What drives the lifecycle-state computation?

## 54. Provisioning policies

1.  What it is: Provisioning policies define the data required to create or update an account on a target, the fields, their sources or defaults, and which must be supplied by a requester. They ensure accounts are created with correct, complete attributes rather than failing at the connector.
2.  Why it matters: They're the difference between smooth automated provisioning and a pile of connector errors for missing required fields. Interviewers probe how you source required attributes and handle target-specific mandatory fields.
3.  How it works: When a provisioning plan is built, the provisioning policy populates account attributes from identity attributes, defaults, generated values, or prompts the requester for anything that can't be derived. The completed plan is then executed by the connector.
4.  Architecture and flow: Provisioning triggered, provisioning policy fills required account attributes (from identity, defaults, or prompts), complete plan passed to the connector, account created/updated.
5.  How I would configure it: Map as many required attributes as possible from identity data or deterministic defaults so provisioning is hands-off, prompt only for what truly can't be derived, and align required fields exactly with the target's mandatory attributes to avoid connector rejections.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Missing required attributes causing provisioning failures, prompting for data that could have been derived, and policies out of sync with the target's real requirements. Expect "how do you ensure accounts are created with all required attributes."
7.  Troubleshooting: Provisioning fails for a missing/invalid attribute, check the provisioning policy mappings against the target's required fields and confirm the identity data or default is actually populated.
8.  Say it in the room: "Provisioning policies guarantee an account is built with all the attributes the target requires, sourced from identity data, defaults, generated values, or a prompt when nothing else works. The goal is hands-off provisioning, so I derive as much as possible and only prompt for the irreducible. The common failure is a policy out of sync with the target's mandatory fields, which shows up as connector rejections, so I align them precisely."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How do you minimize requester prompts during provisioning? What happens when a target requires an attribute you can't derive? How do you keep policies aligned with target requirements?

## 55. Lifecycle events

1.  What it is: Lifecycle events (ISC) and their IIQ equivalents are the configured triggers that fire automated actions when an identity's lifecycle state changes, joiner, mover, leaver, and custom states like leave-of-absence. They connect "something changed about this person" to "do these provisioning and notification actions."
2.  Why it matters: They're the automation engine of lifecycle management. Interviewers probe trigger conditions, ordering, and how you avoid events firing incorrectly or in the wrong sequence.
3.  How it works: You define the event's trigger (an attribute transition, a date, a state change) and its actions (provisioning, notifications, workflows). When identity processing detects the matching transition, the event fires its actions. Careful trigger definitions prevent misfires.
4.  Architecture and flow: Attribute/state transition detected during processing, matching lifecycle event triggers, configured actions (provision, notify, workflow) execute.
5.  How I would configure it: Define precise, mutually-clear triggers so only the intended transition fires each event, order actions sensibly, handle the LOA/return and future-dated cases explicitly, and test with realistic transitions including edge cases like rehire.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Overlapping triggers firing the wrong event, future-dated events firing early/late, and LOA handling. Expect "how do you handle a leave of absence" and "how do you prevent an event from firing incorrectly."
7.  Troubleshooting: An event fired wrongly or not at all, inspect the trigger condition versus the actual attribute transition and the processing logs. Misfires are almost always imprecise trigger definitions.
8.  Say it in the room: "Lifecycle events are the triggers that turn an identity state change into automated action, provision, notify, run a workflow. The craft is in precise trigger definitions so exactly the intended transition fires each event, and in handling the awkward states, leave of absence and return, future-dated hires and terminations, and rehire. I test with realistic transitions, because most lifecycle bugs are overlapping or imprecise triggers rather than the actions themselves."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How do you handle leave-of-absence and return? How do you prevent two events from firing on one transition? How do future-dated events work?

## 56. Access requests and approvals

1.  What it is: The self-service workflow where users request access from a catalog and it routes through approvals before being provisioned. It's how access gets granted outside of birthright, with governance (approval, SoD checks, audit) built in.
2.  Why it matters: It's the day-to-day face of IGA for end users and the control point for granting discretionary access. Interviewers probe catalog design, approval routing, SoD-at-request, and keeping approvals meaningful rather than rubber stamps.
3.  How it works: The user picks an item (entitlement, role, or app access) from the request catalog; the request routes to approvers (manager, entitlement owner, app owner), SoD and policy checks run (preventively), and on approval a provisioning plan fulfills it. Everything is logged for audit.
4.  Architecture and flow: User requests from catalog, approval routing (manager/owner), SoD/policy preventive check, provisioning on approval, audit record.
5.  How I would configure it: Build a business-friendly catalog with described, risk-rated items and roles so people request job functions, route approvals to people with real context, run SoD preventively at request, and escalate/expire stalled approvals. Make high-risk items require stronger approval.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Catalog items nobody understands, approvals rubber-stamped, SoD not checked at request time, and stalled approvals blocking work. Expect "how do you keep approvals meaningful" and "how does SoD fit into the request flow."
7.  Troubleshooting: An approved request didn't provision, check the provisioning plan and connector result. An inappropriate grant got through, check whether SoD ran at request and whether the approver had context.
8.  Say it in the room: "Access requests are the governed, self-service path for discretionary access: request from a catalog, route to approvers with real context, run SoD preventively, then provision on approval, all audited. I make the catalog business-friendly with described, risk-rated items and roles so people request a job function rather than cryptic groups, and I require stronger approval for high-risk items. The failure I design against is rubber-stamp approvals, which I fight with context and risk-based routing."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How do you keep approvals from being rubber stamps? Where does SoD checking happen in the request flow? How do you handle a stalled or ignored approval?

## 57. Roles, profiles and entitlements in SailPoint

1.  What it is: The access model layers. Entitlements are the raw grants (a group, a permission). In IIQ, business roles map to job functions and contain or require IT roles that bundle entitlements; ISC uses roles and access profiles (an access profile bundles entitlements for one source, a role bundles access profiles). It's how SailPoint abstracts raw access into assignable business units.
2.  Why it matters: Good role/profile design is what makes birthright provisioning, requests, and certifications scale; bad design is role explosion. Interviewers probe the modeling layers and how you prevent role bloat, a signature IGA judgment call.
3.  How it works: Entitlements aggregate from sources. Access profiles (ISC) group entitlements per application. Roles group profiles (ISC) or IT roles/entitlements (IIQ) and are assigned either automatically (by attributes/membership criteria) or by request. Assignment flows the bundled entitlements to the identity and on to provisioning.
4.  Architecture and flow: Entitlements aggregated, grouped into access profiles/IT roles, bundled into business roles, assigned by criteria or request, provisioned to targets.
5.  How I would configure it: Use a two-layer model, business roles assigned from HR attributes mapping to profiles/IT roles that carry entitlements, mine roles from real access patterns, and resist creating a role per exception. Govern role membership and role content separately.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Role explosion, overlapping roles confusing certification, and roles drifting from the org. Expect "how do you prevent role explosion" and "explain access profiles versus roles in ISC."
7.  Troubleshooting: Someone has unexpected access, determine whether it came from a role/profile assignment or a direct grant outside the model. Direct grants bypassing roles are both the cause and the governance problem.
8.  Say it in the room: "SailPoint layers access: entitlements are the raw grants, access profiles bundle them per application, and roles bundle profiles into business functions assigned by attribute or request. The whole point is to abstract thousands of entitlements into a manageable set of assignable roles. I use a two-layer model and mine roles from actual access patterns, and the discipline that keeps it healthy is not spawning a new role for every exception, because role explosion makes certification meaningless."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Access profiles versus roles in ISC, how do they relate? How do you prevent role explosion? Business roles versus IT roles, what's the split?

## 58. Access certifications

1.  What it is: The campaign-driven periodic review in SailPoint where reviewers confirm or revoke access. IIQ and ISC generate certifications scoped to identities, applications, roles, or entitlements, route items to reviewers, and auto-remediate revocations.
2.  Why it matters: It's the detective control auditors focus on and a core IGA deliverable. Interviewers probe campaign design, reviewer selection, and how you make reviews meaningful and actually remediate, not just record decisions.
3.  How it works: You define the campaign type and scope, assign reviewers (manager, app owner, role owner), and launch. Reviewers approve or revoke each item; revocations generate provisioning actions to actually remove access; completion and decisions are captured as audit evidence. Risk and change can target what gets reviewed.
4.  Architecture and flow: Campaign scoped and launched, items routed to reviewers, decisions captured, revocations auto-provisioned, evidence retained.
5.  How I would configure it: Give reviewers context (described, risk-rated entitlements), scope by risk and recent change rather than certifying everything, default to revoke on no-decision, stagger campaigns to avoid fatigue, and confirm revocations are fulfilled. Use micro-certifications for event-driven reviews (e.g. after a mover).
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Rubber-stamping from lack of context, oversized campaigns, and revocations decided but never fulfilled. Expect "how do you stop rubber-stamping" and "what certification types does SailPoint support."
7.  Troubleshooting: Revocations not taking effect, check the remediation/provisioning path and connector result. Low-quality decisions usually mean missing entitlement descriptions or the wrong reviewer.
8.  Say it in the room: "Access certifications are SailPoint's detective control, scoped campaigns where the right reviewer confirms or revokes access and revocations auto-provision. The whole value is making them meaningful, so I give reviewers described, risk-rated entitlements, scope by risk and recent change instead of reviewing everything, default to revoke on no-decision, and verify revocations are actually fulfilled, not just recorded. For movers I use event-driven micro-certifications so review happens when access changes, not months later."
9.  Likely follow-ups: How do you prevent rubber-stamping? What certification types does SailPoint support and when do you use each? How do you make sure revocations are actually fulfilled?

## 59. SoD policies and violations

1.  What it is: In SailPoint, SoD policies encode conflicting access (role- or entitlement-based) and the engine detects violations on the identity cube, both preventively at request and detectively during refresh and certification. It's the toxic-combination control implemented in the IGA tool.
2.  Why it matters: SoD is a top compliance and audit concern (SOX especially), and interviewers probe the preventive-versus-detective split, how you handle unavoidable violations with mitigating controls, and how you avoid false positives.
3.  How it works: You define policies as conflicting sets of roles or entitlements. Preventive enforcement runs during access request (block or require extra approval). Detective enforcement runs during identity refresh and certifications, flagging existing violations. Violations route to remediation or get an approved mitigating control with an owner and expiry.
4.  Architecture and flow: SoD policy of conflicts, checked at request (preventive) and on refresh/certification (detective), violations remediated or mitigated with documented controls.
5.  How I would configure it: Define conflict rules with business and audit, enforce preventively where possible, detect everywhere, tune rules to avoid false positives, and build a clean mitigating-control process with ownership and review dates. Make violations visible to the right approvers.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Rules too broad (noise) or too narrow (missed conflicts), violations introduced via direct grants, and mitigating controls that never expire. Expect "a legitimate need creates an SoD violation, what do you do."
7.  Troubleshooting: A violation slipped through, check whether access was granted outside the governed request path (so preventive never ran) or the rule didn't cover that pair. Both are common and distinct fixes.
8.  Say it in the room: "SailPoint implements SoD as policies of conflicting roles or entitlements, enforced preventively at request and detectively on refresh and certification. When the business genuinely needs a conflicting combination, I don't ignore it, I attach a documented mitigating control with an owner and an expiry. The failure modes I tune for are noisy over-broad rules and violations sneaking in through direct grants that bypass the preventive check."
9.  Likely follow-ups: Preventive versus detective SoD in SailPoint? How do you handle an unavoidable violation? How do you keep SoD rules from being noisy?

## 60. Workflows, rules and custom connectors

1.  What it is: The extensibility layer. IIQ uses workflows (orchestrate multi-step processes), BeanShell rules (inject custom logic at defined points), and custom connectors (integrate systems with no standard connector). ISC favors transforms, the API, and SaaS connectivity with far less arbitrary code.
2.  Why it matters: This is where IGA engineers earn their salary and also where projects go to die if over-customized. Interviewers probe where rules plug in, when to customize versus configure, and the maintainability trade-off.
3.  How it works: Workflows chain steps (approvals, provisioning, notifications) with branching. Rules run at specific hooks, correlation, BeforeProvisioning/AfterProvisioning, build-map, field value, to tailor behavior. Custom connectors implement the connector interface (or use the generic web services/JDBC connectors) for non-standard targets.
4.  Architecture and flow: Standard processing hits a hook, a rule or workflow injects custom logic, provisioning plans flow through standard or custom connectors to the target.
5.  How I would configure it: Configure before you code, use out-of-box connectors and declarative options first. When custom logic is needed, keep rules small, well-documented, and centralized, version-control everything, and prefer the generic web services connector over a bespoke one. Treat customization as technical debt to minimize.
6.  Where it breaks and what interviewers probe: Rule sprawl that nobody can maintain, logic duplicated across hooks, and brittle custom connectors. Expect "when do you write a rule versus configure" and "where do BeforeProvisioning rules fit."
7.  Troubleshooting: Custom logic misbehaves, identify the exact hook/rule that ran (task results and logs), and check inputs/outputs at that point. Connector issues isolate to the connector's logs and the target's API behavior.
8.  Say it in the room: "SailPoint's extensibility is workflows for orchestration, BeanShell rules at specific hooks like correlation and Before/AfterProvisioning, and custom or generic connectors for odd targets. My rule of thumb is configure before code, every custom rule is maintenance debt. When I must customize I keep rules small, documented, centralized, and version-controlled, and I reach for the generic web services connector before building a bespoke one. ISC pushes you toward transforms and the API, which I actually prefer for maintainability."
9.  Likely follow-ups: When do you write a rule versus configure out of the box? Where do BeforeProvisioning and AfterProvisioning rules run? How do you integrate a target with no standard connector?

## 61. Provisioning plans

1.  What it is: A provisioning plan is SailPoint's internal representation of the exact account and attribute changes to make on targets, the normalized "what to do" (create account, add entitlement, set attribute, disable) that connectors then execute. It's the bridge between a governance decision and a connector action.
2.  Why it matters: Understanding plans is how you reason about and debug provisioning. Interviewers probe how a decision (role assignment, request approval, lifecycle event) becomes concrete account operations, and how partial failures are handled.
3.  How it works: When access changes, SailPoint compiles a provisioning plan of account requests and attribute requests. Rules (like BeforeProvisioning) can modify the plan.
